========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of my life? Oh, no. I used to have a memory like a steel trap. I could recall the Dewey numbers for the entire European history section without even looking. No, this fog... and just feeling really low, it's really only been happening most days for about three months now. 

It started right around the time I began getting everything ready to leave the library. Which, let me tell you, the retirement paperwork the county gives you is just ridiculous. They sent me this packet that must have been fifty pages long. You'd think people working with books would know how to summarize, right? And they ask for all these records from decades ago. My brother-in-law, he retired from the postal service a few years back and he didn't have to do half of this. He just bought an RV and drove down to Florida. I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. We had a donation once from a lady who kept her books in a damp basement and... well, it was a tragedy. 

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, just the last three months or so for the fogginess and the low mood. Not my whole life.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.

Not my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these problems started because of getting ready to retire?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the retirement stuff didn't help. It's a lot of pressure, you know? But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Our mother used to make us eat boiled cabbage every Tuesday, and I swore when I moved out I’d never eat a leafy green again if I didn't want to. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really know if the retirement caused it. Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. 

But anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.

the library.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... 

But, uh, no, no medical problems lately. Just the usual aches and pains.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, forgetting things... it's more like my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes at work. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. 

But, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.

But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Our mother used to make us eat boiled cabbage every Tuesday, and I swore when I moved out I’d never eat a leafy green again if I didn't want to. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really know if the retirement caused it. Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... \n\nBut, uh, no, no medical problems lately. Just the usual aches and pains.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, forgetting things... it's more like my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes at work. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. 

But anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.

Anyway, I can't even keep my place in my notes anymore. Or decide what simple thing to do next. It’s like, should I sort the returns or check the pull list? And speaking of returns, people have been bringing back books in terrible condition. I found a coffee stain on a first edition last Tuesday. Just no respect for the materials... but yeah, I just can't seem to focus on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of trouble focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Most of my life? Oh, no. I used to have a memory like a steel trap. I could recall the Dewey numbers for the entire European history section without even looking. No, this fog... and just feeling really low, it's really only been happening most days for about three months now. \n\nIt started right around the time I began getting everything ready to leave the library. Which, let me tell you, the retirement paperwork the county gives you is just ridiculous. They sent me this packet that must have been fifty pages long. You'd think people working with books would know how to summarize, right? And they ask for all these records from decades ago. My brother-in-law, he retired from the postal service a few years back and he didn't have to do half of this. He just bought an RV and drove down to Florida. I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. We had a donation once from a lady who kept her books in a damp basement and... well, it was a tragedy. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, just the last three months or so for the fogginess and the low mood. Not my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these problems started because of getting ready to retire?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the retirement stuff didn't help. It's a lot of pressure, you know? But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Our mother used to make us eat boiled cabbage every Tuesday, and I swore when I moved out I’d never eat a leafy green again if I didn't want to. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really know if the retirement caused it. Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel this low mood is mainly because of your upcoming retirement?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days lately I sit at the kitchen table feeling like the color has gone out of everything, and even my old watercolor books just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, the retirement is coming up, sure, but... no, I don't think it's just that. It's more like... most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just drained out of everything. Even my old watercolor books, the ones I used to love flipping through, they just make me feel heavy now instead of interested. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. 

But, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. It's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... my mind just feels foggy lately. Anyway, I can't even keep my place in my notes anymore. Or decide what simple thing to do next. It’s like, should I sort the returns or check the pull list? And speaking of returns, people have been bringing back books in terrible condition. I found a coffee stain on a first edition last Tuesday. Just no respect for the materials... but yeah, I just can't seem to focus on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of trouble focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Most of my life? Oh, no. I used to have a memory like a steel trap. I could recall the Dewey numbers for the entire European history section without even looking. No, this fog... and just feeling really low, it's really only been happening most days for about three months now. \n\nIt started right around the time I began getting everything ready to leave the library. You'd think people working with books would know how to summarize, right? And they ask for all these records from decades ago. My brother-in-law, he retired from the postal service a few years back and he didn't have to do half of this. He just bought an RV and drove down to Florida. I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. Not my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these problems started because of getting ready to retire?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the retirement stuff didn't help. It's a lot of pressure, you know? But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Well, I suppose... I mean, this whole heavy, foggy feeling, it's been happening most days for about three months now. It started right around the time I began doing the paperwork to leave the library. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. 

But, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.

She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. It's right next to this old ceramic rooster my wife bought at a flea market in upstate New York. We took a trip up there about ten years ago, right around autumn. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. 

But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. I mean, honestly, I just don't know. Maybe? It's just really hard to tell right now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... my mind just feels foggy lately. Like, I'm trying to look at my old cataloguing notes at work—I've been at the library for years, you know. Started out just shelving books back in '98. They had this beautiful old Dewey Decimal system card catalog that they were finally phasing out. It was a shame, really, all that solid oak furniture going to the basement. Anyway, I can't even keep my place in my notes anymore. Or decide what simple thing to do next. It’s like, should I sort the returns or check the pull list? And speaking of returns, people have been bringing back books in terrible condition. I found a coffee stain on a first edition last Tuesday. Just no respect for the materials... but yeah, I just can't seem to focus on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of trouble focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Most of my life? Oh, no. I used to have a memory like a steel trap. I could recall the Dewey numbers for the entire European history section without even looking. No, this fog... and just feeling really low, it's really only been happening most days for about three months now. \n\nIt started right around the time I began getting everything ready to leave the library. Which, let me tell you, the retirement paperwork the county gives you is just ridiculous. They sent me this packet that must have been fifty pages long. You'd think people working with books would know how to summarize, right? And they ask for all these records from decades ago. My brother-in-law, he retired from the postal service a few years back and he didn't have to do half of this. He just bought an RV and drove down to Florida. I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. Not my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these problems started because of getting ready to retire?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the retirement stuff didn't help. It's a lot of pressure, you know? But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Our mother used to make us eat boiled cabbage every Tuesday, and I swore when I moved out I’d never eat a leafy green again if I didn't want to. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really know if the retirement caused it. Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... \n\nBut, uh, no, no medical problems lately. Just the usual aches and pains.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, forgetting things... it's more like my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes at work. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low mood is mainly because of your upcoming retirement?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the retirement is coming up, sure, but... no, I don't think it's just that. It's more like... most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just drained out of everything. Even my old watercolor books, the ones I used to love flipping through, they just make me feel heavy now instead of interested. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. It's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen around the time this started that felt especially stressful?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, this whole heavy, foggy feeling, it's been happening most days for about three months now. It started right around the time I began doing the paperwork to leave the library. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. It's just really hard to tell right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. It's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen around the time this started that felt especially stressful?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, this whole heavy, foggy feeling, it's been happening most days for about three months now. It started right around the time I began doing the paperwork to leave the library. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I... I do think it's stronger than just being annoyed about forms. I mean, like I was saying before, most days I just sit there at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just gone out of the world. Even those old watercolor books I mentioned, they just make me feel so heavy now. I used to spend hours looking at the different techniques, especially the wet-on-wet style. It's so tricky to get right because the paper buckles if you don't stretch it first. You have to soak the paper in the tub and then tape it down to a board with this special gummed tape. If you don't get the edges perfectly flat, the water pools in the middle and ruins the whole wash. I ruined so many good sheets of Arches cold-press paper trying to learn that. Arches is the best, you know, made in France. They've been making paper since the 1400s or something like that. Imagine a company being around that long. Now businesses just pop up and disappear in a year. Like that little bakery that opened on Elm Street last spring. They had these wonderful almond croissants, but they closed by Thanksgiving. Couldn't afford the rent, I guess. The landlord there is notorious for jacking up the prices the second a lease is up. 

But, uh, yes. The heavy feeling. It just doesn't seem like it's just about the retirement paperwork. It feels much deeper than that.

Anyway, I can't even keep my place in my notes anymore. Or decide what simple thing to do next. It’s like, should I sort the returns or check the pull list? And speaking of returns, people have been bringing back books in terrible condition. I found a coffee stain on a first edition last Tuesday. Just no respect for the materials... but yeah, I just can't seem to focus on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of trouble focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Most of my life? Oh, no. I used to have a memory like a steel trap. I could recall the Dewey numbers for the entire European history section without even looking. No, this fog... and just feeling really low, it's really only been happening most days for about three months now. \n\nIt started right around the time I began getting everything ready to leave the library. Which, let me tell you, the retirement paperwork the county gives you is just ridiculous. They sent me this packet that must have been fifty pages long. You'd think people working with books would know how to summarize, right? And they ask for all these records from decades ago. My brother-in-law, he retired from the postal service a few years back and he didn't have to do half of this. He just bought an RV and drove down to Florida. I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. We had a donation once from a lady who kept her books in a damp basement and... well, it was a tragedy. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, just the last three months or so for the fogginess and the low mood. Not my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these problems started because of getting ready to retire?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the retirement stuff didn't help. It's a lot of pressure, you know? But I don't know if I'd say it *started* because of that. I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, my sister thinks it's just my diet. She’s been on this new kick, eating only raw vegetables and seeds. Says it clears the mind. She brought over this kale smoothie last week that tasted exactly like lawn clippings. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? Our mother used to make us eat boiled cabbage every Tuesday, and I swore when I moved out I’d never eat a leafy green again if I didn't want to. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really know if the retirement caused it. Just... that's when I noticed it getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. It's... it's strange. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color has just gone out of everything. Even my old watercolor books—I used to love looking through those. The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... \n\nBut, uh, no, no medical problems lately. Just the usual aches and pains.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, forgetting things... it's more like my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes at work. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low mood is mainly because of your upcoming retirement?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the retirement is coming up, sure, but... no, I don't think it's just that. It's more like... most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just drained out of everything. Even my old watercolor books, the ones I used to love flipping through, they just make me feel heavy now instead of interested. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. It's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen around the time this started that felt especially stressful?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, this whole heavy, foggy feeling, it's been happening most days for about three months now. It started right around the time I began doing the paperwork to leave the library. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. It's right next to this old ceramic rooster my wife bought at a flea market in upstate New York. We took a trip up there about ten years ago, right around autumn. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. I mean, honestly, I just don't know. Maybe? It's just really hard to tell right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low mood is much stronger than you would expect from dealing with the retirement paperwork?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do think it's stronger than just being annoyed about forms. I mean, like I was saying before, most days I just sit there at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just gone out of the world. It's so tricky to get right because the paper buckles if you don't stretch it first. You have to soak the paper in the tub and then tape it down to a board with this special gummed tape. If you don't get the edges perfectly flat, the water pools in the middle and ruins the whole wash. I ruined so many good sheets of Arches cold-press paper trying to learn that.
