Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. It's right next to this old ceramic rooster my wife bought at a flea market in upstate New York. We took a trip up there about ten years ago, right around autumn. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. I mean, honestly, I just don't know. Maybe? It's just really hard to tell right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low mood is much stronger than you would expect from dealing with the retirement paperwork?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do think it's stronger than just being annoyed about forms. I mean, like I was saying before, most days I just sit there at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just gone out of the world. Even those old watercolor books I mentioned, they just make me feel so heavy now. I used to spend hours looking at the different techniques, especially the wet-on-wet style. It's so tricky to get right because the paper buckles if you don't stretch it first. You have to soak the paper in the tub and then tape it down to a board with this special gummed tape. If you don't get the edges perfectly flat, the water pools in the middle and ruins the whole wash. I ruined so many good sheets of Arches cold-press paper trying to learn that. Arches is the best, you know, made in France. They've been making paper since the 1400s or something like that. Imagine a company being around that long. Now businesses just pop up and disappear in a year. Like that little bakery that opened on Elm Street last spring. They had these wonderful almond croissants, but they closed by Thanksgiving. Couldn't afford the rent, I guess. The landlord there is notorious for jacking up the prices the second a lease is up. \n\nBut, uh, yes. The heavy feeling. It just doesn't seem like it's just about the retirement paperwork. It feels much deeper than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling especially worried or overwhelmed about retiring?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not exactly worried. I mean, it's a big change, sure, but I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed by the idea of not working. Honestly, the library hasn't been the same since they put in those new self-checkout kiosks anyway. They took out the old wooden circulation desk—solid oak, it was beautiful—and replaced it with these sleek, white plastic counters that look like they belong in a spaceship, not a library. And the scanners are always breaking down. People get so frustrated trying to scan their own books, and they end up waving them around and the machine starts beeping this awful, high-pitched noise. It sounds exactly like the smoke detector in my hallway when the battery is dying. You know that chirp? It always happens at three in the morning, without fail. I had to drag a stepstool out of the pantry just last week to change it. The stool is one of those folding metal ones with the rubber steps, but the rubber is starting to peel off the edges. I keep meaning to glue it back down with some contact cement, but you really have to do that outside because the fumes are so strong. They'll give you a headache in five minutes flat. 

But, um... retiring? No, I don't think I'm overwhelmed by the retirement itself. I just... I don't really have those kinds of worried thoughts about it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... my mind just feels foggy lately. Like, I'm trying to look at my old cataloguing notes at work—I've been at the library for years, you know. Started out just shelving books back in '98. They had this beautiful old Dewey Decimal system card catalog that they were finally phasing out. It was a shame, really, all that solid oak furniture going to the basement. Anyway, I can't even keep my place in my notes anymore. Or decide what simple thing to do next. It’s like, should I sort the returns or check the pull list? And speaking of returns, people have been bringing back books in terrible condition. I found a coffee stain on a first edition last Tuesday. Just no respect for the materials... but yeah, I just can't seem to focus on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of trouble focusing for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Most of my life? I don't think I'd ever like Florida, though. Too humid. It ruins the binding on good books, the glue just melts right off the spine if you aren't careful. I poured it right down the sink when she wasn't looking. You can't just drink grass and expect to feel better, right? The techniques they used in the 19th century were just incredible, especially the botanical illustrations. There was this one artist, Redouté, who painted roses for Marie Antoinette, I think. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... \n\nBut, uh, no, no medical problems lately. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low mood is mainly because of your upcoming retirement?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the retirement is coming up, sure, but... no, I don't think it's just that. It's more like... most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just drained out of everything. Even my old watercolor books, the ones I used to love flipping through, they just make me feel heavy now instead of interested. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. It's right next to this old ceramic rooster my wife bought at a flea market in upstate New York. We took a trip up there about ten years ago, right around autumn. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. I mean, honestly, I just don't know. Maybe? It's just really hard to tell right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low mood is much stronger than you would expect from dealing with the retirement paperwork?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do think it's stronger than just being annoyed about forms. I mean, like I was saying before, most days I just sit there at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just gone out of the world. Even those old watercolor books I mentioned, they just make me feel so heavy now. I used to spend hours looking at the different techniques, especially the wet-on-wet style. It's so tricky to get right because the paper buckles if you don't stretch it first. You have to soak the paper in the tub and then tape it down to a board with this special gummed tape. If you don't get the edges perfectly flat, the water pools in the middle and ruins the whole wash. I ruined so many good sheets of Arches cold-press paper trying to learn that. Arches is the best, you know, made in France. They've been making paper since the 1400s or something like that. Imagine a company being around that long. Now businesses just pop up and disappear in a year. Like that little bakery that opened on Elm Street last spring. They had these wonderful almond croissants, but they closed by Thanksgiving. Couldn't afford the rent, I guess. The landlord there is notorious for jacking up the prices the second a lease is up. \n\nBut, uh, yes. The heavy feeling. It just doesn't seem like it's just about the retirement paperwork. It feels much deeper than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or overwhelmed about retiring?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly worried. I mean, it's a big change, sure, but I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed by the idea of not working. Honestly, the library hasn't been the same since they put in those new self-checkout kiosks anyway. They took out the old wooden circulation desk—solid oak, it was beautiful—and replaced it with these sleek, white plastic counters that look like they belong in a spaceship, not a library. And the scanners are always breaking down. People get so frustrated trying to scan their own books, and they end up waving them around and the machine starts beeping this awful, high-pitched noise. It sounds exactly like the smoke detector in my hallway when the battery is dying. You know that chirp? It always happens at three in the morning, without fail. I had to drag a stepstool out of the pantry just last week to change it. The stool is one of those folding metal ones with the rubber steps, but the rubber is starting to peel off the edges. I keep meaning to glue it back down with some contact cement, but you really have to do that outside because the fumes are so strong. They'll give you a headache in five minutes flat. \n\nBut, um... retiring? No, I don't think I'm overwhelmed by the retirement itself. I just... I don't really have those kinds of worried thoughts about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It just doesn't seem like it's just about the retirement paperwork. It feels much deeper than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or overwhelmed about retiring?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly worried. I mean, it's a big change, sure, but I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed by the idea of not working. Honestly, the library hasn't been the same since they put in those new self-checkout kiosks anyway. They took out the old wooden circulation desk—solid oak, it was beautiful—and replaced it with these sleek, white plastic counters that look like they belong in a spaceship, not a library. And the scanners are always breaking down. People get so frustrated trying to scan their own books, and they end up waving them around and the machine starts beeping this awful, high-pitched noise. It sounds exactly like the smoke detector in my hallway when the battery is dying. You know that chirp? It always happens at three in the morning, without fail. I had to drag a stepstool out of the pantry just last week to change it. The stool is one of those folding metal ones with the rubber steps, but the rubber is starting to peel off the edges. I keep meaning to glue it back down with some contact cement, but you really have to do that outside because the fumes are so strong. They'll give you a headache in five minutes flat. \n\nBut, um... retiring? No, I don't think I'm overwhelmed by the retirement itself. I just... I don't really have those kinds of worried thoughts about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual daily activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I have let the lecture plans, travel notes, and even simple household paperwork pile up because I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, I've just let everything pile up. The lecture plans I used to work on, my travel notes, even just simple household paperwork... it's all just sitting there because I can't seem to organize myself the way I used to. I used to be so organized, you know? I had this filing cabinet, an old metal one I painted forest green to match the trim in the study. I used those hanging folders with the little plastic tabs. You have to write really small to fit the labels in there, but I always used a fine-point Sharpie. The ultra-fine ones, actually. They're great, but if you leave the cap off for even a minute, they dry out completely. My grandson got hold of one a few months ago and drew all over the back of the sofa. It's a floral pattern, so it blends in a little, but you can still see these black squiggles if the light hits it right. We tried everything to get it out—rubbing alcohol, hairspray, even this special upholstery cleaner that smells like strong pine needles. It didn't do a thing. 

But, uh, yes, the daily activities. I just... I can't seem to stay on top of things like I used to. It's all just piling up.

It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. Or maybe it was Josephine? Napoleon's wife. Anyway, they had this way of layering the pigments that you just don't see anymore. Everything now is digital. My nephew showed me this app on his tablet where you just tap a screen and it fills in the colors for you. What's the point of that? Where's the skill? It's like painting by numbers but worse because you don't even get your hands dirty. I used to get so much joy out of just studying the brushstrokes in my books. But now... now I look at them and I just feel heavy. Like I can't even muster up the energy to care about it. So, yeah, I guess things I used to enjoy just don't do it for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I feel heavy and tired, but I'm not... I wouldn't do anything to myself. I still have to sort out my pension, for one thing. And I have to take care of Barnaby. He's my cat. A big orange tabby. I found him behind the library about five years ago, just a tiny kitten then. Someone had left him in a cardboard box right next to the recycling bins. Can you imagine? People can be so careless. It's like the time someone returned a first edition copy of Hemingway with coffee stains on the cover. Just no respect for things that matter. Anyway, Barnaby is very particular about his food. He only eats this one brand of pate, the chicken and liver flavor. If I try to give him the turkey, he just looks at me and walks away. Cats are funny like that. They train you, really. You think you own them, but they run the house. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has three poodles and they just yap all day long. I don't know how she stands it. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, about hurting myself. No, no. I'd never do that. Barnaby would be devastated.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I'd say that. Like I was saying earlier about sitting at the kitchen table... it's just this constant feeling lately. Like the color has just been drained out of everything, you know? Most of the day, every day, just... heavy. Even looking at my watercolor books, which usually cheer me right up. Did I tell you I found a rare print at a garage sale once? It was tucked inside an old encyclopedia set. People just throw the most amazing things away without even looking. It was a beautiful study of a fern, probably from the early 1900s. I had it custom framed. The framer was this young guy who tried to talk me into a modern metal frame, but I said no, it needs mahogany. You have to respect the era of the piece. Metal frames always look so cold, like you're in a hospital or something. My dentist has these awful metal frames in his waiting room with pictures of teeth in them. Why would anyone want to look at giant teeth while they're waiting to get a cavity filled? It just makes you more nervous. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just feel down. Sitting there, looking at my books, and it just feels heavy instead of interesting. Most of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: No, no recent illnesses or injuries. I've been physically fine, really. Well, I mean, my knees ache sometimes when it rains, but that's just getting older, isn't it? My grandfather used to say he could predict the weather better than the meteorologist on Channel 4 just by how his left knee felt. He was a carpenter, worked with his hands his whole life. Built this beautiful cedar chest for my grandmother when they got married. It had this intricate carving on the lid—a ship, because they met on a ferry. People don't make furniture like that anymore. Now you just go to one of those big warehouse stores and buy a box of particle board and some Allen wrenches. I tried putting together a bookshelf from one of those places once, and I swear they left out half the screws. I ended up using wood glue and hoping for the best. It's holding up my paperbacks, but I wouldn't trust it with the hardcovers. Hardcovers are just so much better, don't you think? The weight of them, the smell of the paper... \n\nBut, uh, no, no medical problems lately. Just the usual aches and pains.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, forgetting things... it's more like my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes at work. I'll be looking right at the Dewey Decimal numbers, and it's just a blur, and I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. It's so frustrating because cataloguing used to be my favorite part of the job. You get to see all the new books before anyone else. Last week we got this stunning pictorial history of the transcontinental railroad. The photographs were incredible, all those workers laying track in the middle of nowhere. It makes you think about how hard life was back then. They didn't have heavy machinery, just pickaxes and dynamite. My great-uncle actually worked on the railroad, not building it, but as a conductor. He had this beautiful pocket watch, gold-plated, with a chain that he wore on his vest. He used to let me listen to it tick when I was a boy. It had a very distinct sound, not like the cheap digital watches people wear today that just beep at you. I hate things that beep. My microwave beeps five times when it's done, and it drives me absolutely up the wall. Why does it need to beep five times? Once is plenty. \n\nBut, um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the fog. Yeah, it's just hard to think straight or make decisions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my appetite has definitely dropped off. I actually have to remind myself to eat these days. My wife has been noticing it too. She points out that I leave my rice and soup half-finished, which is unusual for me because I would never waste a meal before. I grew up with a mother who lived through the Depression, so you cleaned your plate, no matter what. She used to make this beef stew that would last us three days. The first night was great, but by day three, the carrots were just mush. Still, you ate it. She kept all her recipes on these little index cards in a metal tin. I actually found that tin in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter coats. It still smelled like paprika and old paper. I tried making her meatloaf from one of the cards, but it didn't taste the same. I think she always added a little something extra that she never wrote down. People do that, you know? They keep their secrets. Like the head librarian at work, Mrs. Higgins. She has this secret system for organizing the rare periodicals, and she won't tell anyone how it works. If she retires, we're going to be completely lost. I told her she needs to write it down, but she just taps her nose and smiles. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Food just doesn't seem appealing, so I end up leaving half of it sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low mood is mainly because of your upcoming retirement?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the retirement is coming up, sure, but... no, I don't think it's just that. It's more like... most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just drained out of everything. Even my old watercolor books, the ones I used to love flipping through, they just make me feel heavy now instead of interested. I used to paint a little, you know, just small landscapes, nothing fancy. Mostly barns and fences. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he had this beautiful old red barn that was leaning slightly to the left. I painted it maybe a dozen times trying to get the angle just right. He sold the property last year to some developer, and they tore the barn down in an afternoon. Just like that, a hundred years of history gone, replaced by these identical beige houses with no yards. It's a shame how everything has to be new and identical now. There's no character left in the world. Even the new books at the library, the bindings feel cheap, like they'll fall apart after three reads. Not like the old cloth-bound ones we have in the archives. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not just the retirement. It's just this heavy, gray feeling all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen around the time this started that felt especially stressful?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, this whole heavy, foggy feeling, it's been happening most days for about three months now. It started right around the time I began doing the paperwork to leave the library. There are so many forms, you wouldn't believe it. Pension options, healthcare continuation... it's a mountain of paperwork. The HR lady, Brenda, she gave me this massive folder, one of those thick accordion ones with the elastic band. The band snapped the first time I tried to open it, hit me right in the knuckle. Hurt like the dickens. It reminded me of when I was a kid playing with those wooden balsa airplanes, the ones with the rubber band propellers. You'd wind them up too tight and the rubber band would snap, and that was the end of your airplane. We used to buy them at the five-and-dime downtown. That whole block is gone now, of course. It's a parking garage for the hospital. Which is useful, I suppose, but it's just concrete. It's ugly. They don't build things with any aesthetic sense anymore. Everything is just functional and gray. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yeah, so right around when I started dealing with all that, about three months ago, that's when this low mood really set in and hasn't let up.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement paperwork were no longer an issue, do you think you would still feel this same heavy, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know... I'm really not sure. It's hard to separate the two right now, you know? Because the paperwork is just always sitting there on my kitchen counter. It's right next to this old ceramic rooster my wife bought at a flea market in upstate New York. We took a trip up there about ten years ago, right around autumn. The leaves were just turning, absolutely beautiful reds and golds. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman named... oh, what was it... Beatrice! That's it. She made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. Real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. You can always taste the difference when people cut corners with margarine. It leaves this weird film on the roof of your mouth. My sister used to bake with margarine in the eighties because everyone thought butter was bad for your heart, but her chocolate chip cookies always turned out flat as a pancake. Just terrible. You couldn't even dunk them in milk properly without them turning to mush in two seconds. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, if I'd still feel this way without the retirement forms. I mean, honestly, I just don't know. Maybe? It's just really hard to tell right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low mood is much stronger than you would expect from dealing with the retirement paperwork?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I do think it's stronger than just being annoyed about forms. I mean, like I was saying before, most days I just sit there at the kitchen table and it feels like all the color has just gone out of the world. Even those old watercolor books I mentioned, they just make me feel so heavy now. I used to spend hours looking at the different techniques, especially the wet-on-wet style. It's so tricky to get right because the paper buckles if you don't stretch it first. You have to soak the paper in the tub and then tape it down to a board with this special gummed tape. If you don't get the edges perfectly flat, the water pools in the middle and ruins the whole wash. I ruined so many good sheets of Arches cold-press paper trying to learn that. Arches is the best, you know, made in France. They've been making paper since the 1400s or something like that. Imagine a company being around that long. Now businesses just pop up and disappear in a year. Like that little bakery that opened on Elm Street last spring. They had these wonderful almond croissants, but they closed by Thanksgiving. Couldn't afford the rent, I guess. The landlord there is notorious for jacking up the prices the second a lease is up. \n\nBut, uh, yes. The heavy feeling. It just doesn't seem like it's just about the retirement paperwork. It feels much deeper than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or overwhelmed about retiring?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly worried. I mean, it's a big change, sure, but I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed by the idea of not working. Honestly, the library hasn't been the same since they put in those new self-checkout kiosks anyway. They took out the old wooden circulation desk—solid oak, it was beautiful—and replaced it with these sleek, white plastic counters that look like they belong in a spaceship, not a library. And the scanners are always breaking down. People get so frustrated trying to scan their own books, and they end up waving them around and the machine starts beeping this awful, high-pitched noise. It sounds exactly like the smoke detector in my hallway when the battery is dying. You know that chirp? It always happens at three in the morning, without fail. I had to drag a stepstool out of the pantry just last week to change it. The stool is one of those folding metal ones with the rubber steps, but the rubber is starting to peel off the edges. I keep meaning to glue it back down with some contact cement, but you really have to do that outside because the fumes are so strong. They'll give you a headache in five minutes flat. \n\nBut, um... retiring? No, I don't think I'm overwhelmed by the retirement itself. I just... I don't really have those kinds of worried thoughts about it.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, I've just let everything pile up. The lecture plans I used to work on, my travel notes, even just simple household paperwork... it's all just sitting there because I can't seem to organize myself the way I used to. I used to be so organized, you know? I had this filing cabinet, an old metal one I painted forest green to match the trim in the study. I used those hanging folders with the little plastic tabs. You have to write really small to fit the labels in there, but I always used a fine-point Sharpie. The ultra-fine ones, actually. They're great, but if you leave the cap off for even a minute, they dry out completely. My grandson got hold of one a few months ago and drew all over the back of the sofa. It's a floral pattern, so it blends in a little, but you can still see these black squiggles if the light hits it right. We tried everything to get it out—rubbing alcohol, hairspray, even this special upholstery cleaner that smells like strong pine needles. It didn't do a thing. \n\nBut, uh, yes, the daily activities. I just... I can't seem to stay on top of things like I used to. It's all just piling up."
  }
